Decay of correlations and thermodynamic limit for the circular Riesz gas
Jeanne Boursier

Abstract
. We investigate the thermodynamic limit of the circular long-range Riesz gas, a system of particles interacting pairwise through an inverse power kernel. We show that after rescaling, so that the typical spacing of particles is of order 1 1 , the microscopic point process converges as the number of points tends to infinity, to an infinite volume measure Riesz s , β \mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta} . This convergence result is obtained by analyzing gaps correlations, which are shown to decay in power-law with exponent 2 − s 2-s . Our method is based on the analysis of the Helffer-Sjöstrand equation in its static form and on various discrete elliptic regularity estimates.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Setting of the problem

The circular Riesz gas

This paper aims to study an interacting particles system on the circle
\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, named circular Riesz gas. Given a parameter
s>0
, the Riesz
s
-kernel on
\mathbb{T}
is defined by

(1.1)

g_{s}:x\in\mathbb{T}\mapsto\lim_{n\to\infty}\Bigr(\sum_{k=-n}^{n}\frac{1}{|x+k|^{s}}-\frac{2}{1-s}n^{1-s}\Bigr).

Note that for
s\in(0,1)
,
g_{s}
is the fundamental solution of the fractional Laplace equation

(1.2)

(-\Delta)^{\frac{1-s}{2}}g_{s}=c_{s}(\delta_{0}-1),

where
(-\Delta)^{\frac{1-s}{2}}
is the fractional Laplacian on
\mathbb{T}
. Let us now endow
\mathbb{T}
with the natural order
x<y
if
x=x^{\prime}+k
,
y=y^{\prime}+k^{\prime}
with
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in[0,1)
and
x^{\prime}<y^{\prime}
, allowing one to define the set of ordered configurations

D_{N}=\{X_{N}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{T}^{N}:x_{2}-x_{1}<\ldots<x_{N}-x_{1}\}.

Now consider the pairwise energy

(1.3)

\mathcal{H}_{N}:X_{N}\in D_{N}\mapsto N^{-s}\sum_{i\neq j}g_{s}(x_{i}-x_{j}).

Finally, the circular Riesz gas at inverse temperature
\beta>0
corresponds to the probability measure

(1.4)

\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}=\frac{1}{Z_{N,\beta}}e^{-\beta\mathcal{H}_{N}(X_{N})}\mathds{1}_{D_{N}}(X_{N})\mathrm{d}X_{N}.

One of the main motivations for studying one-dimensional Riesz gases stems from random matrix theory. The Riesz kernel is indeed given for
s=0
, up to a multiplicative constant, by
-\log|x|
on
\mathbb{R}
and by
-\log|\sin(x/2)|
on
\mathbb{T}
. The Gibbs measure associated to such a logarithmic kernel is called 1D log-gas, or
\beta
-ensemble on
\mathbb{R}
or circular
\beta
-ensemble on
\mathbb{T}
. As observed by Dyson
[
Dys62
]
, for some special values of
\beta
, namely
\beta\in\{1,2,4\}
, the
\beta
-ensemble matches the joint law of the
N
eigenvalues of symmetric/hermitian/symplectic random matrices with independent Gaussian entries. Due to this connection, there are numerous probabilistic results on
\beta
-ensembles concerning for instance fluctuations in the bulk and at the edge, correlations of points and gaps, infinite volume limit, relaxation of the Langevin dynamics, high temperature limit, etc.

The one-dimensional Riesz gas is therefore a natural extension of
\beta
-ensembles and also a fundamental model on which to understand the properties of
long-range
particles systems. The interaction (
1.1
) is indeed long-range when
s\in(0,1)
while short-range (or hyper-singular, following the terminology of
[
BHS19
]
) when
s\in(1,+\infty)
. The long-range Riesz gas is to this extent a particularly rich model in which interesting phenomena occur, falling outside the classical theory of statistical mechanics. Riesz gases, as a family of power-law interacting particles systems on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, have also received much attention in the physics literature. Apart from the log and Coulomb cases, which are ubiquitous in both mathematical and physics contexts
[
Ser18
]
, Riesz gases have been found out to be natural models in solid state physics,
ferrofluids, elasticity, see for instance
[
Maz11
,
BBDR05
,
CDR09
,
Tor16
]
. We refer to the nice review
[
Lew22
]
which presents a comprehensive account of the literature with many open problems.

The first-order asymptotic of long-range Riesz gases is governed by a mean-field energy functional, which prescribes the macroscopic distribution of particles
[
CGZ14
,
Ser15
]
, corresponding in our circular setting (
1.4
) to the uniform measure of the circle. In
[
Bou21
]
, we have investigated the fluctuations of the system and shown that gaps (large spacing between particles) fluctuate much less than for i.i.d variables and much more than in the log-gas case. We have additionally established a central limit theorem for linear statistics with test-functions of very poor regularity, which can for instance be applied to characteristic functions of intervals, thus proving rigorously the predictions of the physics literature
[
Lew22
,
SKA+21
]
. The purpose of this very paper is to investigate another class of problems, related to the question of decay of correlations. More precisely we work at proving the optimal decay of gap correlations as in
[
EY15
]
which considers this question for
\beta
-ensembles and at proving the uniqueness of the limiting Gibbs state. We will show that after rescaling, chosen so that the typical spacing between particles is of order
1
, the microscopic point process converges in the large
N
limit to a certain point process
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
.

Infinite volume limit

Let
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
be distributed according to (
1.4
). Fix a centering point on
\mathbb{T}
, say
x=0
, and consider the rescaled point configuration

\mathcal{C}_{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{Nx_{i}}\mathds{1}_{|x_{i}|<\frac{1}{4}}.

With a slight abuse of notation,
\mathcal{C}_{N}
can be seen as a random variable on point configurations on
\mathbb{R}
. Our goal is to prove that the law of
\mathcal{C}_{N}
converges as
N
tends to infinity, in a suitable topology, to a certain point process
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
. This property is known in statistical physics as the uniqueness of the Gibbs state and is related to the absence of phase transitions. Note that while the existence of limiting point processes is standard
[
GZ93
,
DHLM21
]
, uniqueness is not expected to hold for general interactions even in dimension one. In the cases of the Gaussian and circular
\beta
-ensembles, a unique limit has been exhibited in the seminal works
[
VV09
,
KVV11
]
and then shown to be universal in the bulk of
\beta
-ensembles for a large class of smooth external potentials in
[
BEY12
,
BEY+14b
]
, see also
[
BFG15
]
. The limiting measure, called the
\mathrm{Sine}_{\beta}
process, can be described using a system of coupled stochastic differential equations
[
VV09
]
or alternatively as the spectrum of an infinite-dimensional random operator
[
VV17
]
. Note that a weaker form of uniqueness for the limit of an averaged microscopic uniqueness was also given in
[
EHL18
]
via a displacement convexity argument. In contrast to the log-gas, the one-dimensional Coulomb gas, i.e with kernel
|x|^{-s}
for
s=-1
, is not translation invariant in infinite volume as proved in
[
Kun74
]
and Gibbs states are therefore non-unique. As a consequence, the proof of uniqueness for the long-range gas should use both convexity arguments and the decay of the interaction. Let us mention that the existence of an accumulation point for the Coulomb gas in dimension
d\geq 2
has been proved in
[
AS21
]
, but the uniqueness of such a limit is still a completely open problem.

Decay of the correlations

The question of the uniqueness of the Gibbs state can be tackled by studying the decay of correlations. Fluctuations of linear statistics being small, the appropriate variables to examine in order to establish a decay statement are not points but rather gaps. In the case of the 1D log-gas, it turns out that the correlation between
N(x_{i+1}-x_{i})
and
N(x_{j+1}-x_{j})
decays in
|i-j|^{-2}
, as proved in
[
EY15
]
. In this paper we give a proof of the optimal decay of gap correlations for the circular Riesz gas, which matches the case
s=0
found in
[
EY15
]
as well as the predictions of the physics literature
[
AM85
,
Mar88
,
Lew22
]
. We will also study the hypersingular case
s>1
.

The Helffer-Sjöstrand equation

The fluctuation-dissipation theorem asserts that the fluctuations at equilibrium of any smooth enough function is related to the associated linear response. This yields in particular a representation of the covariance between two smooth functions in terms of the linear response, which solves a partial differential equation named Helffer-Sjöstrand (H.-S.) equation. This equation appeared in
[
Sjő93a
,
Sjő93b
,
HS94
]
and was more substantially studied in
[
Hel98b
,
Hel98a
,
NS97
]
, where it was used to get decay of correlations, uniqueness and Log-Sobolev inequalities. Many approaches to obtaining decay estimates on solutions in convex cases have been developed in the probability and statistical physics literature. For instance, the Feynmac-Kac representation allows one to express the solution with respect to a random walk in a random environment. This formulation, already pointed out it in
[
HS94
]
,
[
NS97
]
, was used for instance in
[
BM03
,
DGI00
,
GOS01
]
and in the work
[
EY15
]
, which then develops a sophisticated homogenization theory for a system of coupled partial differential equations. There are also some other methods to directly study the solution without the Feynmac-Kac representation, which are based on ideas from stochastic homogenization of elliptic partial differential equations as for instance in
[
NS97
,
AW22
,
DW20
,
Tho23
]
.

As mentioned above, the strategy of
[
EY15
]
requires one to control random walks in random environments, which can be quite technical. The gamble of the present paper is to develop a method relying
only on integration by parts
to treat the long-range Riesz gas with
s\in(0,1)
. We will first consider as a landmark the hypersingular case
s>1
and work with a known distortion argument, used for instance in
[
Hel98a
]
or in older techniques to get the decay of eigenfunctions of Schrödinger operators
[
CT73
]
. We will then adapt the method to the long-range case using substantial new inputs including discrete elliptic regularity estimates. Let us emphasize that as it stands, our method cannot be applied to the logarithmic case since it requires to have nearest-neighbor gaps
all
bounded by a large
N
-dependent constant
O(N^{\varepsilon})
, with overwhelming probability. Note that this was also one of the crucial difficulties in
[
EY15
]
preventing a simple implementation of the techniques of Caffarelli, Chan and Vasseur
[
CCV11
]
.

1.2.
Main results

Our first result, which concerns the correlations between gaps in the long-range regime
s\in(0,1)
, is the following:

Theorem 1

(Decay of the correlations for the long-range Riesz gas)
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
. For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
\xi,\chi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
in
H^{1}
and for each
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
,

(1.5)

|\mathrm{Cov}_{\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}}[\xi(N(x_{i+1}-x_{i})),\chi(N(x_{j+1}-x_{j}))]|\\
\leq C(\beta)(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}}[\xi^{\prime}(x_{i})^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{\infty}e^{-c(\beta)d_{N}(i,j)^{\delta}})(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}}[\chi^{\prime}(x_{j})^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}+|\chi^{\prime}|_{\infty}e^{-c(\beta)d_{N}(i,j)^{\delta}})\frac{1}{d_{N}(i,j)^{2-s-\varepsilon}}.

Moreover, for all
\varepsilon>0
small enough and any
n\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, there exist
i,j
such that
\frac{n}{2}\leq|i-j|\leq n
and

(1.6)

|\mathrm{Cov}_{\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}}[N(x_{i+1}-x_{i}),N(x_{j+1}-x_{j})]|\geq\varepsilon\frac{1}{d_{N}(i,j)^{2-s}}.

Note that
d_{N}
stands for the symmetric distance on
\{1,\ldots,N\}
.

Theorem
1
is the natural extension of
[
EY15
]
, which proves that that for
\beta
-ensembles the correlation between
N(x_{i+1}-x_{i})
and
N(x_{j+1}-x_{j})
decays in
|i-j|^{-2}
. The lower bound (
1.6
) is obtained by using a result from
[
Bou21
]
which gives the leading-order asymptotic of the correlation between
N(x_{i}-x_{1})
and
N(x_{j}-x_{i})
. Theorem
1
is in accordance with the expected decay of the truncated correlation function in the mathematical physics and physics literature, see
[
Lew22
]
.

Let us comment on the norms appearing in (
1.5
). Our method is mainly based on
L^{2}
arguments for a distortion of the Helffer-Sjöstrand equation system which is captured by the
L^{2}
norm of
\xi^{\prime}
and
\chi^{\prime}
. Besides by assuming that
\xi^{\prime}
and
\chi^{\prime}
are uniformly bounded, we can control the solution on a bad event of exponentially small probability by carrying out a maximum principle argument.

Theorem
1
should be compared to the decay of correlations in the short-range case, that we quantify in the next theorem:

Theorem 2

(Decay of correlations for the short-range Riesz gas)
.

Let
s\in(1,+\infty)
. There exists a constant
\kappa>0
such that for all
\xi,\chi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
in
H^{1}
and each
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, we have

(1.7)

|\mathrm{Cov}_{\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}}[\xi(N(x_{i+1}-x_{i})),\chi(N(x_{j+1}-x_{j}))]|\\
\leq C(\beta)(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}}[\xi^{\prime}(x_{i})^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{\infty}e^{-c(\beta)d(i,j)^{\delta}})(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}}[\chi^{\prime}(x_{j})^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}+|\chi^{\prime}|_{\infty}e^{-c(\beta)d(i,j)^{\delta}})\Bigr(\frac{1}{d_{N}(i,j)^{s-\varepsilon}}+\frac{1}{N}\Bigr).

Remark 1.1

(Lagrange multiplier and finite volume correlations)
.

The factor
\frac{1}{N}
reflects correlations due to fact that the total number of points in system is fixed, see
[
EC81
,
PS17
,
BGSRS20
]
. In fact, in the framework of Helffer-Sjöstrand equations, it can be interpreted as a Lagrange multiplier associated to the constraint
\sum_{j=1}^{N}N(x_{j+1}-x_{j})=N
, with the convention that
x_{N+1}=x_{1}
. Interestingly, this correction does not appear in the long-range case (see Theorem
1
).

It would be interesting to establish the rate of decay of correlations in the case
s=1
. We believe that for
s=1
, the situation is similar to the long-range case and that the decay is in
d(i,j)^{-1}\log d(i,j)^{-\kappa}
for some
\kappa>0
. Our next result concerns the limit as
N
tends to infinity of the law of the configuration

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{Nx_{i}}\mathds{1}_{|x_{i}|<\frac{1}{4}},

Since
\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}
is translation invariant, this is equivalent to centering the configuration around any point
x\in\mathbb{T}
. Let
\mathrm{Conf}(\mathbb{R})
be the set of locally finite, simple point configurations in
\mathbb{R}
. Given a Borel set
B\subset\mathbb{R}
, we let
N_{B}:\mathrm{Conf}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{N}
be the number of points lying in
B
. The set
\mathrm{Conf}(\mathbb{R})
is endowed with the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the maps
\{N_{B}:B\ \text{Borel}\}
. A point process is then a probability measure on
\mathrm{Conf}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
distributed according to
\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}
. For all
x\in\mathbb{T}
, denote

(1.8)

\mathbb{Q}_{N,\beta}=\mathrm{Law}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{Nx_{i}}\mathds{1}_{|x_{i}|<\frac{1}{4}}\right)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Conf}(\mathbb{R})).

Theorem 3

(Uniqueness of the limiting measure )
.

Let
s\in(0,1)\cup(1,+\infty)
. There exists a translation invariant point process
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
such that the sequence of point processes
(\mathbb{Q}_{N,\beta})
converges to
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
in the topology of local convergence: for any bounded, Borel and local test function
\phi:\mathrm{Conf}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{N,\beta}}[\phi]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}}[\phi].

Theorem
3
extends the known convergence results for
\beta
-ensembles, see
[
BEY12
,
BEY14a
,
VV09
,
Leb15
,
DHLM21
]
. Additionally we are able to give a quantitative bound on the convergence of
\mathbb{Q}_{N,\beta}(x)
to
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
for smooth test-functions.

Theorem 4

(Quantitative convergence)
.

Let
s\in(0,1)\cup(1,+\infty)
. Let
K\in\{1,\ldots,\frac{N}{2}\}
and
G:\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathbb{R}
in
H^{1}
. Let
F:X_{N}\to D_{N}\mapsto G(N(x_{2}-x_{1}),\ldots,N(x_{K}-x_{K-1})).
Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}
and let us denote
z_{1}=\mathrm{argmin}_{z\in\mathcal{C}}|z_{i}-x|
. Then for all
\varepsilon>0
, there holds

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}}[F]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}}[G(z_{2}-z_{1},\ldots,z_{K}-z_{K-1})]+O_{\beta}\left(N^{-\frac{s}{2}+\varepsilon}\sup|\nabla G|^{2}\right).

Combining the CLT of
[
Bou21
]
and the convergence result of Theorem
3
, we can additionally prove a CLT for gaps and discrepancies under the
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
process. Let
\zeta(s,x)
the Hurwitz zeta function (see for instance
[
Ber72
]
).

Theorem 5

(Hyperuniformity of the
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
process)
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
. Under the process
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
, the sequence of random variables

K^{-\frac{s}{2}}(z_{K}-z_{1}-K)

converges in distribution to
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
as
K
tends to infinity with

\sigma^{2}=\frac{1}{\beta\frac{\pi}{2}s}\mathrm{cotan}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}s\right).

Moreover, the variance of
z_{K}-z_{1}
under
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
may be expanded as

(1.9)

\mathrm{Var}_{\mathrm{Riesz}(\beta)}[z_{k}-z_{1}]=K^{s}\sigma^{2}+o(K^{s}).

In particular, Theorem
5
implies that the fluctuations of the number of points in a given interval under
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
is much smaller than for the Poisson process. In the language of
[
Tor16
]
, this says that
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
is hyperuniform when
s\in(0,1)
. Our techniques, combined with the method of
[
Bou21
]
, can also give a central limit theorem for linear statistics under the
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
process, as done in
[
Leb18
,
Lam21
]
for
\mathrm{Sine}_{\beta}
.

We conclude this set of results by studying the repulsion of the
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
process at
0
. We show that the probability of having two particles very close to each other decays exponentially.

Proposition 1.1
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,\frac{s}{2})
. Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. There exist constants
c(\beta)>0
and
C(\beta)>0
depending on
\alpha
and locally uniformly in
\beta
such that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}}(|z_{i+1}-z_{i}|\geq\varepsilon)\geq 1-C(\beta)e^{-c(\beta)\varepsilon^{-\alpha}}.

1.3.
Related questions and perspective

DLR equations and number-rigidity

Having proved the existence of an infinite volume limit for the circular Riesz gas, a natural question is then to study the
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
process from a statistical physics perspective. The first step in that direction could be to establish the Dubroshin-Landford-Ruelle (DLR) equations for the
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
process as was done for the
\mathrm{Sine}_{\beta}
process in
[
DHLM21
]
. We refer to
[
Geo11
]
for a presentation of DLR equations in the context of lattice gases and to
[
Der19
]
in the context of point processes. A question of interest is then to study the number-rigidity property, recently put forward in
[
GP17
]
, within the family of Riesz gases. Let us recall that a point process is said to be number-rigid whenever given any compact domain of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the knowledge of the exterior determines in a deterministic fashion the number of points inside the domain. Number-rigidity is a quite surprising phenomenon, which has been proved to occur for the 1D log-gas independently in
[
CN18
]
and in
[
DHLM21
]
using DLR equations. The recent work
[
DV21
]
, providing a strategy to rule out number-rigidity, should imply together with the local laws of
[
Bou21
]
, that the
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
process is not number-rigid for
s\in(0,1)
. This would highlight a difference between the log-gas and the long-range Riesz gas for which the effective energy is short-range.

Regularity of the free energy

A natural question is to investigate the regularity with respect to
\beta
of the infinite volume process
\mathrm{Riesz}_{s,\beta}
. A way to address this problem is to study the regularity of the free energy of the infinite Riesz gas, which is defined by

(1.10)

f:\beta\in(0,+\infty)\mapsto\lim_{N\to\infty}-\frac{1}{N}\Bigr(\log Z_{N,\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\beta N^{2-s}\iint g_{s}(x-y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\Bigr).

The existence of such a limit was obtained in
[
LS17
]
for Riesz gases in arbitrary dimension
d\geq 1
with
\max(0,d-2)<s<d
. In dimension one, one expects that no phase transition occurs for the circular Riesz gas and that the free energy is smooth and even analytic. To prove that
f
is twice differentiable, a standard approach is to prove that the rescaled variance of the energy under (
1.4
) converges locally uniformly in
\beta
as
N
tends to infinity. This should be an easy consequence of Theorems
1
and
3
.

Higher dimensions

Because the Hamiltonian of the Riesz gas is not convex in dimension
d\geq 2
, it is not clear how to obtain results on correlations. In fact, even showing local laws in the long-range setting is still open, except in the Coulomb case
s=d-2
tackled in
[
LS17
,
Leb17
]
culminating into the optimal local law result of
[
AS21
]
. Nevertheless, a quantitative estimate on the translation invariance of the 2D Coulomb gas has been recently obtained in the work
[
Leb21
]
, building on a Mermin-Wagner’s-type argument in the spirit of
[
Geo98
]
, see also
[
Tho22
]
for related considerations. It could also be interesting to address this question for the hypersingular Riesz gas
[
HLSS18
]
, which is seemingly more tractable since it resembles the hard-core model as
s
becomes large. For the latter, the translation invariance of the infinite volume Gibbs measures was shown in
[
Ric07
]
by constructing approximate translations avoiding collapses between particles.

1.4.
Outline of the proofs

As mentioned, the heart of the paper is about the analysis of a partial differential equation related to the correlations of the Riesz gas. Given a typical Gibbs measure
\mathrm{d}\mu=e^{-H(X_{N})}\mathrm{d}X_{N}
on
D_{N}
(or
\mathbb{R}^{N}
), the well-known fluctuation-dissipation relation asserts that the covariance between any smooth functions
F,G:D_{N}\to\mathbb{R}
may be expressed as

(1.11)

\mathrm{Cov}_{\mu}[F,G]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla G],

where
\nabla\phi
solves

(1.12)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\Delta\phi+\nabla H\cdot\nabla\phi=F-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F]&\text{on }D_{N}\\
\nabla\phi\cdot\vec{n}=0&\text{on }\partial D_{N},\end{array}\right.

One may recognize the operator
\mathcal{L}^{\mu}=-\Delta+\nabla H\cdot\nabla
which is the infinitesimal generator of the Markov semigroup associated to the Langevin dynamics with energy
H
. The Helffer-Sjöstrand equation then corresponds to the equation obtained by differentiating (
1.12
), which reads

(1.13)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{1}^{\mu}\nabla\phi=\nabla F&\text{on }D_{N}\\
\nabla\phi\cdot\vec{n}=0&\text{on }\partial D_{N}\end{array}\right.\quad\text{where}\quad A_{1}^{\mu}:=\nabla^{2}H+\mathcal{L}^{\mu}\otimes I_{N}.

When the Hessian of the energy is uniformly positive-definite, then one can derive by integration by parts a weighted
L^{2}
estimate on
|\nabla\phi|
, which yields a Brascamp-Lieb inequality. Note that a maximum principle argument can also give a uniform bound on
|\nabla\phi|
as seen in
[
HS94
]
.

The Hamiltonian we are interested in is rather a (uniformly) convex function of the gaps than of the points. Henceforth it is very convenient to rewrite Equation (
1.13
) in a new of system of coordinates. We define the change of variables

\mathrm{Gap}_{N}:X_{N}\in D_{N}\mapsto(N|x_{2}-x_{1}|,\ldots,N|x_{N}-x_{1}|)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}

and work on the polyhedron

\mathcal{M}_{N}:=\{(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})\in(\mathbb{R}^{+*})^{N}:y_{1}+\ldots+y_{N}=N\}.

Assume that the measure of interest
\mu
can be written
\mathrm{d}\mu=e^{-H^{\mathrm{g}}\circ\mathrm{Gap}_{N}(X_{N})}\mathds{1}_{D_{N}}(X_{N})\mathrm{d}X_{N}
and that the test-functions in (
1.11
) are of the form
F=\xi\circ\mathrm{Gap}_{N}
and
G=\chi\circ\mathrm{Gap}_{N}
. Set
\nu=\mathrm{Gap}_{N}\#\mu
. Then letting

\mathcal{L}^{\nu}=\nabla H^{\mathrm{g}}\cdot\nabla-\Delta\quad\text{and}\quad A_{1}^{\nu}=\nabla^{2}H^{\mathrm{g}}+\mathcal{L}^{\nu}\otimes I_{N},

one may check that the variance of
F
under
\mu
can also be represented as

\mathrm{Cov}_{\mu}[F,G]=\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla\chi],

where
\nabla\psi
solves

(1.14)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{1}^{\nu}\nabla\psi=\nabla\xi+\lambda(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{N})&\text{on }\mathcal{M}_{N}\\
\nabla\psi\cdot(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{N})=0&\text{on }\mathcal{M}_{N}\\
\nabla\psi\cdot\vec{n}=0&\text{on }\partial\mathcal{M}_{N}.\end{array}\right.

Let us mention that the coefficient
\lambda
in (
1.14
) is the Lagrange multiplier associated to the linear constraint
y_{1}+\ldots+y_{N}=N
. Our focus is to understand how
\partial_{j}\psi
decays when
\nabla\xi=e_{1}
. A first important insight comes from expanding the Hessian of the energy (
1.3
) in gap coordinates, that we denote
\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}
. Using some rigidity estimates obtained in
[
Bou21
]
, one can show that for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
such that

\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}\Bigr(\Bigr|\partial_{ij}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\mathrm{g}}-\frac{1}{1+d_{N}(i,j)^{s}}\Bigr|\geq\frac{1}{1+d_{N}(i,j)^{1+\frac{s}{2}-\varepsilon}}\Bigr)\leq Ce^{-d_{N}(i,j)^{\delta}},

where
d_{N}
stands for the symmetric distance on
\{1,\ldots,N\}
. In other words, the interaction matrix in the system (
1.14
) concentrates around a constant long-range matrix. This already gives a first heuristic to understand the decay of gap correlations stated in Theorem
1
, which is consistent with the decay of
h:=(-\Delta)^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\delta_{0}
(where
(-\Delta)^{\frac{1-s}{2}}
is the fractional Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}
).

Due to the long-range nature of the interaction, the analysis of (
1.14
) is rather delicate. Let us present an idea of the proof in the short-range case
s>1
as it will be the model computation for the long-range case also. To simplify assume that there exist
s>1,c>0,C>0
such that uniformly

(1.15)

\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}\geq c^{-1}\mathrm{Id}\quad\text{with}\quad|\partial_{ij}\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}|\leq\frac{C}{d_{N}(i,j)^{s}}\quad\text{for each $1\leq i,j\leq N$}.

The matrix
\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}
then looks like a diagonally dominant matrix. The idea to obtain a decay estimate on the solution of (
1.14
) is to multiply
\partial_{i}\psi
by
d_{N}(i,1)^{\alpha}
for some well-chosen
\alpha>0
. Let
\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}(\mathbb{R})
be the distortion matrix

\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}=\mathrm{diag}((1+d_{N}(j,1)^{\alpha})_{1\leq j\leq N})

and
v^{\mathrm{dis}}:=\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}\nabla\psi
which solves

\beta(\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}+\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}})v^{\mathrm{dis}}+\mathcal{L}^{\nu}v^{\mathrm{dis}}=e_{1}+\lambda\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{N}),

where
\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}}
is the commutator

(1.16)

\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}}:=\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}^{-1}-\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}.

A first key is that the more
\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}
is diagonal, the more it will commute with diagonal matrices. In fact one can check that for
\alpha<s-\frac{1}{2}
, the commutator (
1.16
) is small compared to the identity, in the sense of quadratic forms. By integration by parts and using the convexity of
\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}
, this entails an
L^{2}
estimate on
\psi^{\mathrm{dis}}
and therefore a hint on the global decay of
\nabla\psi
. This idea of studying a distorted vector-field is well known in statistical physics, see for instance
[
Hel98a
,
CT73
]
. By projecting (
1.14
) in a smaller window we can then improve through a bootstrap argument this first decay estimate.

In the long-range regime
s\in(0,1)
, the above argument no longer works. A natural way of proceeding is to factorize Equation (
1.14
) around the ground state by multiplying the system by a matrix
\mathsf{A}
close to the inverse of the Riesz matrix
\mathbb{H}_{N,s}:=(\frac{\mathds{1}_{i\neq j}}{d_{N}(i,j)^{s}})_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
. A simple construction can ensure that
\mathsf{A}\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\mathrm{g}}
remains uniformly positive-definite but the drawback of the operation is that the differential term
D\psi
can no longer be controlled. The main novelty of the paper is a method based on the comparison of the two distorted norms

(1.17)

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\Bigr[\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{N}(i,1)^{2\alpha}(\partial_{i}\psi)^{2}\Bigr]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\Bigr[\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{N}(i,1)^{2\gamma}|\nabla(\partial_{i}\psi)|^{2}\Bigr],

for well-chosen constants
\alpha>0
and
\gamma>0
. The first step is to derive an elliptic regularity estimate on the solution of (
1.14
). We prove that the solution has a discrete fractional primitive of order
\frac{3}{2}-s
in
L^{2}
(up to some
n^{\kappa\varepsilon}
multiplicative factor) provided
\psi_{i}
decays fast enough. In a second step we will control
|\mathrm{L}_{\gamma}\nabla^{2}\psi|
by
|\mathrm{L}_{\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{1}{4}}\nabla\psi|
(up to a residual term that we do not comment here). The proof uses the distortion argument presented in the short-range case, the elliptic regularity estimate and a discrete Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality. In a third step we control
|\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}\nabla\psi|
by
|\mathrm{L}_{\alpha-\frac{1-s}{2}}\nabla^{2}\psi|
by implementing the factorization trick aforementioned. Combining these two inequalities we deduce that for
\alpha=\frac{3}{2}-s
and
\gamma=1-\frac{s}{2}
, each of the terms in (
1.17
) are small. This gives the optimal global decay on the solution of (
1.14
), which we then seek to localize.

The proof of localization, which allows one to go from (
1.17
) to an estimate on a single
\partial_{i}\psi
, is also quite delicate. Fix an index
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and let

J=\Bigr\{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}:d_{N}(i,j)\leq\frac{1}{2}d_{N}(1,j)\Bigr\}.

Projecting Equation (
1.14
) on the window
J
makes an exterior field appear, which takes the form

(1.18)

\mathrm{V}_{l}:=-\beta\sum_{i\in J^{c}}\partial_{il}\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}\partial_{i}\psi,\quad l\in J.

We then break
\mathrm{V}
into the sum of an almost constant field
\mathrm{V}^{(1)}
(looking like
\mathrm{V}_{j}\sum_{l\in J}e_{l})
and a smaller field
\mathrm{V}^{(2)}
. A key is that the equation (
1.14
) associated to a vector-field proportional to
(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{N})
is much easier to analyze. It indeed admits a mean-field approximation, quite similar to the mean-field approximation of (
1.13
) when
F
is a linear statistics, see
[
Bou21
]
. We then bootstrap the decay of solutions of (
1.14
). Applying the induction hypothesis to bound (
1.18
)
and
to bound the decay of (
1.14
) in the window
J
, one finally gets after a finite number of iterations, the optimal result of Theorem
1
.

The uniqueness of the limiting point process stated in Theorem
3
is then an application of our result on the decay of correlations (in fact stated for slightly more general systems than (
1.14
)). As the existence of an accumulation point of (
1.8
) in the local topology is standard, the problem of convergence of the microscopic process can be rephrased into a uniqueness question.

Our aim is to prove that the sequence (
1.8
) defines, in some informal sense, a Cauchy sequence. To do this, we let
I=\{1,\ldots,n\}
and freeze the points with index in
I^{c}
: given
N,N^{\prime}\gg n
, one seeks to compare the conditional measures
\nu_{n}^{y}:=\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}^{\mathrm{g}}(\cdot\mid y)
and
\nu_{n}^{z}=\mathbb{P}_{N^{\prime},\beta}^{\mathrm{g}}(\cdot\mid z)
where
y\in\mathbb{R}^{N-n}
,
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}-n}
are two exterior configurations verifying
N-(y_{1}+\ldots+y_{N-n})=N^{\prime}-(z_{1}+\ldots+z_{N^{\prime}-n})
. The strategy is then to define a continuous path
\nu(t)
from
\nu_{n}^{y}
to
\nu_{n}^{z}
by linear interpolation of the exterior energies. Let
F
be a test-function
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
depending only on the coordinates
x_{i}
for
\frac{n}{4}\leq i\leq\frac{3n}{4}
. One may write

(1.19)

\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{n}^{z}}[F]-\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{n}^{y}}[F]=\beta\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{Cov}_{\nu(t)}[\nabla F,\nabla E(t)]\mathrm{d}t,

where
E(t)
corresponds to some exterior energy term. By applying the decay of correlations result to the measure
\nu(t)
, we find that (
1.19
) is small, which easily concludes the proof of Theorem
3
.

1.5.
Organization of the paper

•

Section
2
records various preliminary results, such as rigidity estimates on circular Riesz gases and controls on the discrete fractional Laplacian.

•

Section
3
focuses on the well-posedness of the Helffer-Sjöstrand equation and states some of its basic properties.

•

In Section
4
we introduce our distortion techniques to prove the decay of correlations in the long-range case.

•

Section
5
is the heart of the paper. It develops a more involved method to treat the decay of correlations in the long-range setting.

•

Section
6
concludes the proof of uniqueness of the limiting measure of Theorem
3
.

1.6.
Notation

We let
d_{N}
be the symmetric distance on
\{1,\ldots,N\}
defined for each
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
by

d_{N}(i,j)=\min(|j-i|,N-|j-i|).

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we let
|x|
be the Euclidian norm of
x
and for
M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
,
\|M\|
be the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of
M
, i.e

\|M\|=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|Mv|}{|v|}.

We let
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{N})
be the standard orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

We either use the notation
\nabla^{2}f
for the Hessian of a real-valued function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
.

For
A,B\geq 0
, we write
A\leq C(\beta)B
or
A=O_{\beta}(B)
whenever there exists a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
locally uniform in
\beta
(which might depend on
s
) such that
A\leq CB
.

2.
Preliminaries

We begin by recording some useful preliminary results that will be used throughout the paper.

2.1.
Discrepancy estimates

One shall first state a control on the probability of having two particles very close to each other. According to
[
Bou21
, Lem. 4.5]
, the following holds:

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
and
\alpha\in(0,\frac{s}{2})
. There exist constants
C(\beta)>0
and
c(\beta)>0
locally uniform in
\beta
such that for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}(N(x_{i+1}-x_{i})\leq\varepsilon)\leq C(\beta)e^{-c(\beta)\varepsilon^{-\alpha}}.

In addition, in view of
[
Bou21
, Th. 1]
, the fluctuations of large gaps satisfy the following estimate:

Theorem 6

(Near-optimal rigidity)
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
. There exists a constant
C(\beta)
locally uniform in
\beta
such that for all
\varepsilon>0
, setting
\delta=\frac{\varepsilon}{4(s+2)}
, for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and
1\leq k\leq\frac{N}{2}
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}(|N(x_{i+k}-x_{i})-k|\geq k^{\frac{s}{2}+\varepsilon})\leq C(\beta)e^{-c(\beta)k^{\delta}}.

Let us highlight that the variance of
N(x_{i+k}-x_{i})
can in fact be shown to be of order
k^{s}
, together with a central limit theorem, see
[
Bou21
, Cor. 1.1]
.

2.2.
Fractional Laplacian on the circle

In this subsection we justify the expression of the fundamental solution of the fractional Laplace equation on the circle (
1.2
). Recall the Hurwitz zeta function
[
Apo97
]
.

Lemma 2.2

(Fundamental solution)
.

Let
g_{s}
be the solution of (
1.2
). Let
s\in(0,1)
. For all
x\in\mathbb{T}
, we have

(2.1)

g_{s}(x)=\zeta(s,x)+\zeta(s,1-x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\Bigr(\sum_{k=-n}^{n}\frac{1}{|k+x|^{s}}-\frac{2}{1-s}n^{1-s}\Bigr).

Moreover for all
p\geq 1
and all
x\in\mathbb{T}
, we have

g_{s}^{(p)}(x)=(-1)^{p}s\cdots(s+p-1)\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{|x+k|^{s+p}}.

Proof.

We only sketch the main arguments and refer to
[
Bou21
, Sec. 2]
for a more detailed proof. Using the Fourier characterization of the fractional Laplacian and applying the formula

\lambda^{-\frac{1-s}{2}}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{1-s}{2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1-\frac{1-s}{2}}},

valid for all
\lambda>0
, one can express
g_{s}
as

g_{s}(x)=\frac{c_{s}}{\Gamma(\frac{1-s}{2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}(W_{t}(x)-1)\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t^{\frac{1+s}{2}}},

where
W_{t}
is the heat kernel on
\mathbb{T}
, namely

W_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-t|k|^{2}}e^{ikx}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi t}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-\frac{|x-k|^{2}}{4t}}.

The proof of (
2.1
) then follows from Fubini’s theorem which allows one to invert the order of integration and summation.
∎

The kernel
g_{s}
can be identified with a periodic function on
\mathbb{R}
and a crucial consequence of (
2.1
) is that the restriction of this function to
(0,1)
is convex, thus allowing the use of various consequences of convexity, such as concentration and functional inequalities.

2.3.
Discrete and semi-discrete Fourier transforms

The Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms on
\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
are defined by

(2.2)

\mathcal{F}_{d}(f)(\theta)=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}f(n)e^{in\theta},\quad\text{for $f:\{1,\ldots,N\}\to\mathbb{R}$},\quad\theta=\frac{2\pi k}{N},\quad k\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\},

(2.3)

\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}(\phi)(n)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\phi\Bigr(\frac{2\pi k}{N}\Bigr)e^{\frac{-2i\pi k}{N}}\mathrm{d}\theta,\quad\text{for $\phi:\{{2k\pi}/{N}:0\leq k\leq N-1\}\to\mathbb{R}$},\quad n\in\{1,\ldots,N\}.

Recall that for all
f
defined on
\{1,\ldots,N\}
,
f=\mathcal{F}_{d}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{F}_{d}(f)
.

Besides if
f:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
is in
L^{2}
, then the semi-discrete Fourier of
\mathbb{Z}
defined by

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}}(f)(\theta)=\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}f(n)e^{in\theta},\quad\theta\in[0,2\pi],

belongs to
L^{2}([0,2\pi])
and one can recover
f
by the Fourier inverse transform

f=\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}}(f)),

where

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{-1}(\phi)(n)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\phi(\theta)e^{-in\theta}\mathrm{d}\theta,\quad\text{for $\phi\in L^{2}([0,2\pi])$},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

2.4.
Decay of the inverse Riesz kernel

In this subsection one studies the decay of the inverse of the periodic Riesz kernel on
\{1,\ldots,N\}
defined by

(2.4)

g_{N,s}:k\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}\mapsto\lim_{n\to\infty}\Bigr(\sum_{m=-n}^{n}\frac{1}{|k+mn|^{s}}-\frac{2}{1-s}n^{1-s}\Bigr)\mathds{1}_{k\neq 0}.

Denote

(2.5)

\mathbb{H}_{N,s}=(g_{N,s}(j-i))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}(\mathbb{R}).

Let also
g_{N,s}^{-1}:\{1,\ldots,N\}\to\mathbb{R}
be the inverse of the kernel
g_{N,s}
so that

\mathbb{H}_{N,s}^{-1}=(g_{N,s}^{-1}(j-i))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}.

Lemma 2.3

(Decay of the inverse Riesz matrix)
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for each
1\leq i,j\leq N
,

(2.6)

|(\mathbb{H}_{N,s}^{-1})_{i,j}|\leq\frac{C}{1+d(j,i)^{2-s}}.

In addition we have

(2.7)

\Bigr|\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\mathbb{H}_{N,s}^{-1})_{i,1}\Bigr|\leq\frac{C}{N^{1-s}}.

Let us observe that (
2.6
) is consistent with the decay of the fundamental solution of the fractional Laplacian. Indeed the coefficient
(\mathbb{H}_{N,s})^{-1}_{i,1}
is given by the
i
-th coordinate of the solution
v
of the convolution equation
v*g_{N,s}=\delta(1)
on
\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
. The continuous counterpart of this equation is
g_{s}*\psi=\delta_{0}
on the real line and it is well-known that the solution
\psi
decays in
|x|^{-(2-s)}
near the origin.

Proof.

Step 1: the aliasing formula

Let
\psi=g_{N,s}^{-1}
, solution of the convolution equation
g_{N,s}*\psi=\delta(1)
on
\{1,\ldots,N\}
. One can express
\psi
as the solution of

\mathcal{F}_{d}(\psi){\mathcal{F}_{d}(g_{N,s})}=1,

where
\mathcal{F}_{d}
stands for the discrete Fourier transform on
\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
, as defined in (
2.2
). For each
k\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
, let
\theta_{k}=\frac{2\pi k}{N}
. We will prove that the Fourier transform of
\mathcal{F}_{d}(g_{N,s})
is non-vanishing. One can thus consider a function
h\in L^{2}([0,2\pi])
, which interpolates
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\phi)
: for all
\theta\in\{\theta_{0},\ldots,\theta_{N-1}\}
,

h(\theta)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{F}_{d}(g_{N,s})(\theta)}.

The function
h
shall be specified later. Let
\phi:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
in
L^{2}
such that

(2.8)

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\phi)=h.

The point is that one can recover
\psi
from
\phi
: for each
1\leq n\leq N
, there holds

(2.9)

\psi(n)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi(n+kN).

The discrete Fourier transform of the function in the right-hand side of the last display is indeed given for all
\theta\in\{\theta_{0},\ldots,\theta_{N-1}\}
by

\begin{split}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi(n+kN)e^{in\theta}&=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi(n+kN)e^{i(n+kN)\theta}\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\phi(n)e^{in\theta}=h(\theta)=\mathcal{F}_{d}(\psi)(\theta).\end{split}

By Fourier inversion, this concludes the proof of the aliasing formula (
2.9
).

Step 2: discrete and semi-discrete Fourier transform of
g_{N,s}

Let us now compute the discrete Fourier transform of
g_{N,s}
on
\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
. First one can observe that for each
0\leq k\leq N-1
,

(2.10)

\mathcal{F}_{d}(g_{N,s})(\theta_{k})=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n^{s}}e^{in\theta_{k}}+\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n^{s}}e^{-in\theta_{k}}.

Let us emphasize that the above identity is only true for
\theta\in\{\theta_{0},\ldots,\theta_{N-1}\}
. The above sum is related to a well-known function called
periodic zeta function

[
Apo97
]
, defined by

F(x,s)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{e^{2i\pi nx}}{n^{s}},

where
s\in\mathbb{C}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy
\operatorname{Re}(s)>1
if
x
is an integer and
\operatorname{Re}(s)>0
otherwise. One can express (
2.10
) as

\mathcal{F}_{d}(g_{N,s})(\theta_{k})=F(\frac{\theta_{k}}{2\pi},s)+F(-\frac{\theta_{k}}{2\pi},s),\quad\text{for each $0\leq k\leq N-1$.}

Also, when
\operatorname{Re}(s)>0
and
0<x<1
, it is known, see
[
Apo97
]
, that

F(x,s)=\frac{\Gamma(1-s)}{(2\pi)^{1-s}}\Bigr(e^{i\pi\frac{1-s}{2}}\Gamma(1-s,x)+e^{-i\pi\frac{1-s}{2}}\Gamma(1-s,1-x)\Bigr).

Consequently we have the identity
\mathcal{F}_{d}(g_{N,s})=S
on
\{\theta_{0},\ldots,\theta_{N-1}\}
, where

(2.11)

S(\theta)=\frac{2^{s}\Gamma(1-s)}{\pi^{1-s}}\cos(\frac{\pi(1-s)}{2})\Bigr(\Gamma(1-s,\frac{\theta}{2\pi})+\Gamma(1-s,1-\frac{\theta}{2\pi})\Bigr).

One can observe that there exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
,

(2.12)

S(\theta)\geq\frac{c}{|\theta|^{1-s}}.

Step 3: conclusion

We have shown that the discrete Fourier transform of
g_{N,s}
on
\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
does not vanish, thus allowing to use (
2.9
). We now specify
h=S
. Let us define

\phi:n\in\mathbb{Z}\mapsto\int_{0}^{1}S(\theta)e^{-in\theta}\mathrm{d}\theta.

One can check using (
2.10
) that

|\phi(n)|\leq\frac{C}{n^{2-s}}.

Since
\phi\in L^{2}
, by Fourier inversion, one can observe that
\mathcal{F}_{d}(\phi)=S.
Consequently, applying (
2.9
), we find that there exists a constant
C>0
such that for each
1\leq n\leq N
,

|\psi(n)|\leq C\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{|n+kN|^{2-s}}\leq\frac{C}{n^{2-s}},

which proves (
2.6
).
∎

Remark 2.1

(Discrete fractional primitive)
.

In view of (
2.11
) the convolution of
f:\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
with
g_{N,s}
formally corresponds to a fractional primitive of
f
of order
1-s
.

2.5.
Second derivative of the Riesz kernel

One may also introduce the second derivative of the kernel
g_{N,s}
denoted
g_{N,s}^{\prime\prime}:\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
, given for all
U_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
by the equality

\sum_{i,k}g_{N,s}(i-k)u_{i}u_{k}=\sum_{i,k}g_{N,s}^{\prime\prime}(i-k)(u_{i}+\ldots+u_{k})^{2}.

More precisely, we let

(2.13)

g_{N,s}^{\prime\prime}:k\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}\mapsto g_{N,s}(k+1)+g_{N,s}(k-1)-2g_{N,s}(k).

Note that
g_{N,s}^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
with

g_{N,s}^{\prime\prime}(k)=O\Bigr(\frac{1}{d_{N}(k,1)^{2+s}}\Bigr).

Withdrawing the pairs
(i,k)
such that
d_{N}(i,k)\leq K_{0}
for a certain
K_{0}\geq 1
in the sum (
2.13
) provides a way to define, starting from
g_{N,s}
, a non-negative kernel truncated at
K_{0}
. Less intrinsic is the derivative of order
1
of
g_{N,s}
that we nevertheless define for the sequel as

(2.14)

g_{N,s}^{\prime}:i\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}\mapsto g_{N,s}(i+1)-g_{N,s}(i).

3.
The Helffer-Sjöstrand equation

In this section we introduce some standard results on Helffer-Sjöstrand equations. We first recall basic properties valid for a certain class of convex Gibbs measures. We then study an important change of variables and rewrite the Helffer-Sjöstrand in gap coordinates. For the class of Gibbs measures we are interested in, the energy is a convex function of the gaps. This allows one to derive a maximum principle for solutions, which will be a central tool in the rest of the paper.

3.1.
Well-posedness

We start by explaining the principle of Helffer-Sjöstrand representation and give some existence and uniqueness results. The subsection is similar to
[
Bou21
]
and follows partly the presentation of
[
AW22
]
. Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
D_{N}
in the form

\mathrm{d}\mu=e^{-H(X_{N})}\mathds{1}_{D_{N}}(X_{N})\mathrm{d}X_{N},

where
H:D_{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth and convex function. Given a smooth test-function
F:D_{N}\to\mathbb{R}
, we wish to rewrite its variance in a convenient and effective way. Let us recall the integration by parts formula for
\mu
. Let
\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
be the operator acting on
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(D_{N},\mathbb{R})
given by

\mathcal{L}^{\mu}=\nabla H\cdot\nabla-\Delta,

where
\nabla
and
\Delta
are the standard gradient and Laplace operators on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
. The operator
\mathcal{L}^{\mu}
is the generator of the Langevin dynamics associated to the energy
H
of which
\mu
is the unique invariant measure. By integration by parts under
\mu
, for any functions
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(D_{N},\mathbb{R})
such that
\nabla\phi\cdot\vec{n}=0
on
\partial D_{N}
, we can write

(3.1)

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\psi\mathcal{L}^{\mu}\phi]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla\phi].

This formula may be proved by integration by parts under the Lebesgue measure on
D_{N}
.

Assume that the Poisson equation

(3.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{L}^{\mu}\phi=F-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F]&\text{on }D_{N}\\
\nabla\phi\cdot\vec{n}=0&\text{on }\partial D_{N}\end{array}\right.

admits a weak solution in a certain functional space. Then, by (
3.1
), the variance of
F
under
\mu
can be expressed as

\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}[F]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\nabla F\cdot\nabla\phi].

The above identity is called the Helffer-Sjöstrand representation formula. Let us differentiate (
3.2
). Formally, for all
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(D_{N},\mathbb{R})
, we have

\nabla\mathcal{L}^{\mu}\phi=A_{1}\nabla\phi,

where
A_{1}^{\mu}
is the so-called Helffer-Sjöstrand operator given by

A_{1}^{\mu}=\nabla^{2}H+\mathcal{L}^{\mu}\otimes I_{N},

with
\mathcal{L}^{\mu}\otimes I_{N}
acting diagonally on
L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(D_{N},\mathbb{R}))
. Therefore the solution
\nabla\phi
of (
3.2
) formally satisfies

(3.3)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{1}^{\mu}\nabla\phi=\nabla F&\text{on }D_{N}\\
\nabla\phi\cdot\vec{n}=0&\text{on }\partial D_{N}.\\
\end{array}\right.

This partial differential equation is called the Helffer-Sjöstrand equation. Let us now introduce the appropriate functional spaces to make these derivations rigorous. Let us define the norm

\|F\|_{H^{1}(\mu)}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[|\nabla F|^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Let
H^{1}(\mu)
be the completion of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(D_{N})
with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}(\mu)}
. Let also define the norm

\|F\|_{H^{-1}(\mu)}=\sup\{|\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[FG]|:G\in H^{1}(\mu),\|G\|_{H^{1}(\mu)}\leq 1\}.

We denote
H^{-1}(\mu)
the dual of
H^{1}(\mu)
, that is the completion of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(D_{N})
with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{-1}(\mu)}
. We wish to prove that under mild assumptions on
F
, Equation (
3.3
) is well-posed, in the sense of
L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{-1}(\mu))
. Let us now make the following assumptions on
\mu
:

Assumptions 3.1
.

Assume that
\mu
is a probability measure on
D_{N}
written

\mathrm{d}\mu=e^{-H(X_{N})}\mathds{1}_{D_{N}}(X_{N})\mathrm{d}X_{N},

with
H:D_{N}\to\mathbb{R}
in the form

H:X_{N}\mapsto\sum_{i\neq j}\chi(|x_{i}-x_{j}|),

with
\chi:\mathbb{R}^{+*}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\chi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+*},\mathbb{R})\quad\text{and}\quad\chi^{\prime\prime}\geq c>0.

In our applications,
\chi
is often given by
g_{N,s}
or a variant of it and the density of the measure
\mu
is not necessarily bounded from below with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
D_{N}
. Additionally, the measure
\mu
does not satisfies a uniform Poincaré inequality. Due to these limitations, to prove the well-posedness of (
3.3
), we further assume that
F
is a function of the gaps. We denote

(3.4)

\Pi:X_{N}\in D_{N}\mapsto(x_{2}-x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}-x_{1})\in\mathbb{T}^{N-1}.

We also let
\mu^{\prime}
be the push-forward of
\mu
by the map
\Pi
:

\mu^{\prime}=\mu\circ\Pi^{-1}.

We can now state the following well-posedness result:

Proposition 3.1

(Existence and representation)
.

Let
\mu
satisfying Assumptions
3.1
. Let
F\in H^{1}(\mu)
. Assume that
F
is in the form
F=G\circ\Pi
,
G\in H^{1}(\mu^{\prime})
or that
\mu\geq c>0
. Then there exists a unique
\nabla\phi\in L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{1}(\mu))
such that

(3.5)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{1}^{\mu}\nabla\phi=\nabla F&\text{ on }D_{N}\\
\nabla\phi\cdot\vec{n}=0&\text{ on }\partial D_{N},\end{array}\right.

with the first identity being, for each coordinate, an identity on elements of
H^{-1}(\mu)
. Moreover the solution of (
3.5
) is the unique minimizer of the functional

\nabla\phi\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla^{2}H\nabla\phi+|\nabla^{2}\phi|^{2}-2\nabla F\cdot\nabla\phi],

over maps
\nabla\phi\in L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{1}(\mu))
. The variance of
F
may be represented as

(3.6)

\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}[F]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla F]

and the covariance between
F
any function
G\in H^{1}(\mu)
as

\mathrm{Cov}_{\mu}[F,G]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla G].

The identity (
3.6
) is called the Helffer-Sjöstrand formula. The proof of Proposition
3.1
is postponed to the Appendix, see Section
B
.

Remark 3.2

(On the boundary condition)
.

The boundary condition
\nabla\phi\cdot\vec{n}=0
on
\partial D_{N}
means that if
x_{i}=x_{j}
, then
\partial_{i}\phi(X_{N})=\partial_{j}\phi(X_{N})
.

Remark 3.3

(Link to the Monge-Ampère equation)
.

We formally discuss the link between (
3.5
) and the Monge-Ampère equation. Let
F:D_{N}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth test-function. For all
t\geq 0
, consider the measure
\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}=\frac{e^{tF}}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[e^{tF}]}\mathrm{d}\mu
. According to well-known optimal transportation results
[
Bre91
]
, the measure
\mu_{t}
can be written
\mu_{t}=\mu\circ\nabla\Phi_{t}^{-1}
with
\Phi_{t}:D_{N}\to\mathbb{R}
solution of the Monge-Ampère equation

-\log\det D\nabla\Phi_{t}+H\circ\nabla\Phi_{t}-H=tF-\log\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[e^{tF}].

Formally, since
\nu(t)=\mu+t\nu+o(t)
, one expects that
\Phi_{t}=\mathrm{Id}+t\phi+o(t)
. Linearizing the above equation in
t
formally gives

\mathcal{L}^{\mu}\phi=F-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F],

which is the Poisson equation (
3.2
). The boundary condition in (
3.5
) reflects the fact that for all
t\geq 0
,
\nabla\Phi_{t}
maps
D_{N}
on itself.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\mu
satisfying Assumptions
3.1
. Let
v\in L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{-1}(\mu^{\prime}))
such that

v\cdot(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{N})=0.

There exists a unique
\psi\in L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{1}(\mu))
such that

(3.7)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{1}^{\mu}\psi=v&\text{on }D_{N}\\
\psi\cdot\vec{n}=0&\text{on }\partial D_{N}.\end{array}\right.

In addition if
v=\nabla F\in L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{-1}(\mu^{\prime}))
, then the solution of (
3.7
) is given by the solution of (
3.5
).

The proof of Proposition
3.2
is also given in the Appendix.

3.2.
Change of coordinates

In the sequel we will study the decay of correlations in gap coordinates. Define the map

\mathrm{Gap}_{N}:X_{N}\in D_{N}\mapsto(N(x_{2}-x_{1}),\ldots,N(x_{1}-x_{N}))\in\mathcal{M}_{N},

where

\mathcal{M}_{N}=\mathrm{Gap}_{N}(D_{N})=\{Y_{N}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+*})^{N}:y_{1}+\ldots+y_{N}\}.

Since
\mathcal{M}_{N}
is not an open subset of
D_{N}
, Proposition
3.1
should be slightly adapted. Let
\mu
satisfying Assumptions
3.1
and
H^{\mathrm{g}}:\mathcal{M}_{N}\to\mathbb{R}
be such that

H=H^{\mathrm{g}}\circ\mathrm{Gap}_{N}.

Define the generator acting on
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{N},\mathbb{R})
,

\mathcal{L}^{\nu}=\nabla H^{\mathrm{g}}\cdot\nabla-\Delta,

with
\nabla
and
\Delta
the standard gradient and Laplace operator on
\mathcal{M}_{N}
. Also let
A_{1}^{\nu}
be the Helffer-Sjöstrand operator acting on
L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{N},\mathbb{R}))
:

A_{1}^{\nu}=\nabla^{2}H^{\mathrm{g}}+\mathcal{L}^{\nu}\otimes I_{N}.

Let
F:D_{N}\to\mathbb{R}
in the form
F=G\circ\mathrm{Gap}_{N}
with
G:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
smooth. Let us rewrite Equation (
3.5
) in gap coordinates. One can expect that the solution
\nabla\phi
of (
3.5
) can be factorized into
\phi=\psi\circ\mathrm{Gap}_{N}
with
\nabla\psi\in L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{1}(\nu))
. Let us derive some formal computation to conjecture the equation satisfied by
\nabla\psi
. For all
t\geq 0
, let
\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\frac{e^{tG}}{\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[e^{tG}]}\mathrm{d}\nu
. In view of Remark
3.3
, we wish to find a map
\nabla\psi\in L^{1}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{1}(\nu))
such that in a certain sense,

(3.8)

\nu\circ(\mathrm{Id}+t\nabla\psi)=\nu_{t}+o(t).

Since
\nu
and
\nu_{t}
are both measures on
\mathcal{M}_{N}
, one can observe that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{i}\psi=0
. It is standard the the Gibbs measure
\nu_{t}
is the minimizer of the functional

\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M}_{N})\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[H^{\mathrm{g}}+tG]+\mathrm{Ent}(P),

where
\mathrm{Ent}
stands for the entropy on
\mathcal{M}_{N}
. Equation (
3.8
) is compatible with the variational characterization if
\nabla\psi
minimizes

\nabla\psi\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{g}}\nabla\psi+|\nabla^{2}\psi|^{2}-2\nabla G\cdot\nabla\psi],

over maps
\nabla\psi\in L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{1}(\nu))
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{i}\psi=0
and
\nabla\psi\cdot\vec{n}=0
on
\partial\mathcal{M}_{N}
. The Lagrange equation associated for the minimality of
\nabla\psi
reads

A_{1}^{\nu}\nabla\psi=\nabla G+\lambda(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{N}),

where
\lambda:\mathcal{M}_{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function. We now state this result in the following proposition:

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\mu
satisfying Assumptions
3.1
. Let
F\in H^{1}(\mu)
in the form
F=G\circ\mathrm{Gap}_{N}
with
G\in H^{1}(\nu)
. There exists a unique
\nabla\psi\in L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{1}(\nu))
solution of

(3.9)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{1}^{\nu}\nabla\psi=\nabla G+\lambda(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{N})&\text{on }\mathcal{M}_{N}\\
\nabla\psi\cdot(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{N})=0&\text{on }\mathcal{M}_{N}\\
\nabla\psi\cdot\vec{n}=0&\text{on }\partial\mathcal{M}_{N},\end{array}\right.

with
\lambda
satisfying

(3.10)

\lambda=\frac{1}{N}(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{N})\cdot(\nabla^{2}H^{\mathrm{g}}\nabla\psi-\nabla G).

The variance of
F
can be represented as

\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}[F]=\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\nabla G\cdot\nabla\psi].

Furthermore,
\nabla\psi
is the unique minimizer of

\nabla\psi\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla^{2}H^{\mathrm{g}}\nabla\psi+|\nabla^{2}\psi|^{2}-2\nabla G\cdot\nabla\psi],

over maps
\nabla\psi\in L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{1}(\nu))
such that
\nabla\psi\cdot(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{N})=0
.

The proof of Proposition
3.3
is postponed to the Appendix, see Section
B
.

Remark 3.4
.

There are several manners to factorize the energy (
1.3
) since we are working on the circle. We choose the more natural one and set

\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}:Y_{N}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}\mapsto N^{-s}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N/2}g_{N,s}(y_{i}+\ldots y_{i+k})(2\mathds{1}_{k\neq N/2}+\mathds{1}_{k=N/2}).

One may check that for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and
Y_{N}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}
,

(3.11)

\partial_{i}\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}(Y_{N})=\sum_{k=1}^{N/2}\sum_{l:i-k<l\leq i}N^{-(1+s)}g_{s}^{\prime}\Bigr(\frac{y_{i}+\ldots+y_{i+l}}{N}\Bigr)(2\mathds{1}_{k\neq N/2}+\mathds{1}_{k=N/2})

and for each
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and
Y_{N}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}
,

(3.12)

\partial_{ij}\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}(Y_{N})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k,k^{\prime}\leq N/2\\
|k-k^{\prime}|\leq N/2\end{subarray}}N^{-(1+s)}g_{N,s}^{\prime\prime}\Bigr(\frac{y_{i-k}+\ldots+y_{j+k^{\prime}}}{N}\Bigr)(2\mathds{1}_{|k-k^{\prime}|\neq N/2}+\mathds{1}_{|k-k^{\prime}|=N/2}).

Recall that under the Gibbs measure (
1.4
), for large
k
, the spacing
N(x_{i+k}-x_{i})
concentrates around
k
. The expression (
3.12
) then tells us that the Hessian of the energy in gap coordinates concentrates around a constant matrix with off-diagonal entries decaying in
d(i,j)^{-s}
, similar to (
2.5
).

3.3.
The Brascamp-Lieb inequality

We now recall the Brascamp-Lieb inequality, a basic concentration inequality for strictly convex log-concave measures
[
BL02
]
. In our context, the measure
\mu
is not strictly log-concave, but its pushforward
\nu
is, therefore allowing one to bound the variance of any smooth function of the gaps in the following way:

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset D_{N}
be a convex domain with a piecewise smooth boundary. Let
F=G\circ\mathrm{Gap}_{N}
with
G\in H^{1}(\nu)
. There holds

\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}[F\mid\mathcal{A}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\nabla F\cdot(\nabla^{2}H)^{-1}\nabla F\mid\mathcal{A}].

3.4.
Localization

In this subsection we record a crucial convexity Lemma, which is due to Brascamp, see
[
BL02
]
. This lemma is based on the Brascamp-Lieb inequality for log-concave measures on
D_{N}
, originally derived in
[
BL76
]
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, see also Lemma
3.4
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\mu
be a measure on
D_{N}
in the form
\mathrm{d}\mu=e^{-H}\mathrm{d}X_{N}
, with
H
smooth enough. On
D_{N}
let us introduce the coordinates
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
y=(x_{n+1},\ldots,x_{N})
. Assume that
H
may be written in the form
H(x,y)=H_{1}(x)+H_{2}(x,y)
with
\nabla^{2}H_{2}
non-negative. Let
\widetilde{\mu}
be the push forward of
\mu
by the map
X_{N}\mapsto(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. Then, the measure
\widetilde{\mu}
may be written in the form

\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\mu}(x)=e^{-\widetilde{H}(x)}\mathrm{d}x
,
with

\widetilde{H}(x)=-\log\int e^{-H(x,y)}\mathrm{d}y

and
\widetilde{H}
satisfies

\nabla^{2}\widetilde{H}\geq\nabla^{2}H_{1}.

Moreover, we have

(3.13)

\partial_{i}\widetilde{H}(x)=\partial_{i}H(x)-\mathbb{E}_{\mu(\cdot\mid x)}[\partial_{i}H_{2}],\quad\text{for each}\quad 1\leq i\leq n,\quad x\in D_{n},

(3.14)

\partial_{ij}\widetilde{H}(x)=\partial_{ij}H(x)-\mathrm{Cov}_{\mu(\cdot\mid x)}[\partial_{i}H_{2},\partial_{j}H_{2}],\quad\text{for each}\quad 1\leq i,j\leq n,\quad x\in D_{n}.

3.5.
Maximum principle

In this subsection we derive a useful maximum principle, which allows one to bound the supremum of the
L^{2}
norm of the solution in presence of a uniformly convex Hamiltonian. This maximum principle is fairly standard on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, see for instance
[
HS94
, Sec. 10]
. We adapt the proof to make it work on
D_{N}
and
\mathcal{M}_{N}
. A more subtle analysis could perhaps permit to treat general convex domains.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\mu
satisfying Assumptions
3.1
and
\nu=\mathrm{Gap}_{N}\#\mu
. Assume additionally that
\lim_{x\to 0}\chi^{\prime}(x)=-\infty
. Let
\mathsf{M}:\mathcal{M}_{N}\to\mathcal{S}_{N}(\mathbb{R})
be a measurable map. Assume that there exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
U_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

(3.15)

U_{N}\cdot\mathsf{M}U_{N}\geq c|U_{N}|^{2}.

Let
v\in L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{1}(\nu))
and
\psi\in L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{1}(\nu))
be the solution of

(3.16)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{M}\psi+(\mathcal{L}^{\nu}\otimes I_{N})\psi=v&\text{on }\mathcal{M}_{N}\\
\psi\cdot\vec{n}=0&\text{on }\partial\mathcal{M}_{N}.\end{array}\right.

Then
\psi
satisfies the following uniform estimate:

(3.17)

\sup|\psi|\leq c^{-1}\sup|v|.

We give a proof of Proposition
3.6
via stochastic flow following the approach of
[
CFG22
, Th. 2.1]
.

Proof.

We wish to give a Feynman-Kac representation for solutions of (
3.16
). Let

X_{t}^{x}=x-\int_{0}^{t}\nabla H^{\mathrm{g}}(X_{s}^{x})\mathrm{d}s+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}B_{t}.

Note that since
\lim_{x\to 0}\xi^{\prime}(x)=-\infty
, the dynamics is conservative: for all
x\in D_{N}
, the process
X_{t}^{x}
does not hit the boundary of
\mathcal{M}_{N}
a.s. Let us denote
A
the operator from
L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{1}(\nu))
to
L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{-1}(\nu))

A=\mathsf{M}+\mathcal{L}^{\nu}\otimes I_{N}.

Applying the spectral theorem for bounded self-adjoint operator to
A^{-1}
, which has a positive spectral gap, allows one to write

\psi=\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-tA}v\mathrm{d}t.

By Itô’s formula, one may represent
e^{-tA}v
as

(e^{-tA}v)(x)=\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[v(X_{t}^{x})e^{-\int_{0}^{t}\mathsf{M}(X_{s}^{x})\mathrm{d}s}],\quad\text{for all $x\in\mathcal{M}_{N}$}.

Using the uniform convexity assumption of (
3.15
), one then gets

\sup|e^{-tA}v|\leq\sup|v|e^{-tc}

Integrating the last display with respect to
t
finally gives the estimate (
3.17
).
∎

The proof of Proposition
3.6
is an adaptation in a more involved case of a known maximum principle for the Helffer-Sjöstrand equation, see for instance
[
HS94
]
.

Let us emphasize that the above proof crucially relies on the fact that
\lim_{x\to 0}\chi^{\prime}(x)=-\infty
. We now give the standard Gaussian concentration lemma for uniformly log-concave measures on convex bodies.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\mu
satisfying Assumptions
3.1
and
\nu=\mathrm{Gap}_{N}\#\mu
. Let
c_{N}
be the constant in (
3.15
). Let
\mathcal{A}\subset D_{N}
be a convex domain with a piecewise smooth boundary. Let
F=G\circ\mathrm{Gap}_{N}
with
G\in H^{1}(\nu)
. For all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\log\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[e^{tF}\mid\mathcal{A}]\leq t\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F\mid\mathcal{A}]+\frac{t^{2}}{2c_{N}}\sup_{\mathcal{A}}|\nabla G|^{2}.

Lemma
3.7
can be derived using Log-Sobolev inequality and Herbst argument. When a measure
\mu
is uniformly log-concave on a convex domain on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it follows from the Bakry-Emery criterion
[
BÉ85
]
that
\mu
satisfies a Log-Sobolev inequality.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\mu
be a uniformly log-concave measure on a convex domain of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, with a convexity constant larger than
c>0
. Then
\mu
satisfies the Log-Sobolev inequality with constant
2c^{-1}
.

3.6.
Concentration inequality for divergence free functions

If
\mu
is of the form of Assumptions
3.1
,
\mu
is not uniformly log-concave and one cannot directly apply Lemma
3.7
. However, one can observe that for all
U_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
such that
u_{1}+\ldots+u_{N}=0
,

U_{N}\cdot\nabla^{2}HU_{N}\geq c_{N}\sum_{i\neq j}(N(u_{i}-u_{j}))^{2}=c_{N}(N-1)\sum_{i=1}^{N}u_{i}^{2}.

Using this observation and the particular structure of
\mu
, one can give a concentration estimate for divergence free functions
F
, i.e for
F
verifying
\partial_{1}\phi+\ldots+\partial_{N}\phi=0
. We now state the crucial concentration result found in
[
BEY12
, Lem. 3.9]
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\mu
satisfying Assumptions
3.1
. Assume that
\chi^{\prime\prime}\geq c_{N}
. Let
I\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}
,
\mathrm{card}(I)=K
. Let
F\in H^{1}(\mu)
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{i}F=0
and
\partial_{i}F=0
for each
i\in I^{c}
. We have

(3.18)

\mathrm{Var}_{\mu}[F]\leq\frac{1}{(K-1)c_{N}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[|\nabla F|^{2}].

Furthermore, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\log\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[e^{tF}]\leq t\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F]+\frac{t^{2}}{2(K-1)c_{N}}\sup|\nabla F|^{2}.

We refer to
[
BEY12
, Lem. 3.9]
for a proof, see also
[
Bou21
, Lem. 3.13]
for a transcription.

4.
Decay of correlations for the HS Riesz gas

This section considers the hypersingular Riesz gas, i.e the Riesz gas with the kernel (
2.1
) for a parameter
s>1
. We show that the covariance between
N(x_{i+1}-x_{j})
and
N(x_{j+1}-x_{j})
decays at least in
d_{N}(i,j)^{-(s+1)}
. To this end we will be studying the Helffer-Sjöstrand equation in gap coordinates (
3.9
). Advantaged by that the Hessian of the energy in gap coordinates has typically summable entries, one may implement a simple distortion argument inspired from
[
Hel98a
]
to obtain sharp decay estimates.

4.1.
Study of a commutator

Let us begin by introducing the distortion argument. Given
s>1
, let
\nu
be the measure (
1.4
) in gap coordinates or a slight variant of it. We will be studying the equation

(4.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{1}^{\nu}\nabla\psi=e_{1}+\lambda(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{N})&\text{on }\mathcal{M}_{N}\\
\nabla\psi\cdot(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{N})=0&\text{on }\mathcal{M}_{N}\\
\nabla\psi\cdot\vec{n}=0&\text{on }\partial\mathcal{M}_{N}.\end{array}\right.

By Remark
3.4
, if
\nu=\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}^{\mathrm{g}}
there exists an event of overwhelming probability on which the Hessian of the energy in gap coordinates decays in
d_{N}(i,j)^{-s}
away from the diagonal. Following
[
Hel98a
]
, the idea is to study the equation satisfied by
\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}\nabla\psi
, where
\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}
stands for the following distortion matrix:

(4.2)

\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}=\mathrm{diag}(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{N}),\quad\text{where $\gamma_{i}=1+d_{N}(i,i_{0})^{\alpha}$ for each $1\leq i\leq N$}.

Let us denote

\psi^{\mathrm{dis}}=\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}\nabla\psi\quad\in L^{2}(\{1,\ldots,N\},H^{1}(\nu)).

One can check that
\psi^{\mathrm{dis}}
solves

A_{1}^{\nu}\nabla\psi+\beta\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}}\nabla\psi=e_{1}+\lambda\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{N}),\quad\text{where}\quad\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}}:=\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}\nabla^{2}{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}^{-1}-{\mathcal{H}}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}.

Note that when
\mathrm{H}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}(\mathbb{R})
is a matrix with off-diagonal entries decaying fast enough, then the commutator
\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}\mathrm{H}\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}^{-1}-\mathrm{H}
is in some sense small compared to the identity, as shown in the next lemma.

Lemma 4.1

(Commutation lemma)
.

Let
s>1
and
\mathrm{H}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}(\mathbb{R})
. Assume that there exists a constant
\varepsilon>0
such that

(4.3)

|\mathrm{H}_{i,j}|\leq\frac{N^{\varepsilon}}{1+d_{N}(i,j)^{s}},\quad\text{for each $1\leq i,j\leq N$}.

Let
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{1}{2})
and
\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}
be as in (
4.2
). There exist constants
C>0
and
c>0
such that for all
\varepsilon_{0}>0
small enough, letting
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{0}}{\min(s-1,s-\frac{1}{2}-\alpha)}
, we have that for all
U_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

(4.4)

|U_{N}\cdot(\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}\mathrm{H}\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}^{-1}-\mathrm{H})U_{N}|\leq\frac{1}{2}N^{-\varepsilon_{0}}|U_{N}|^{2}+CC_{N}^{\kappa}|U_{N}|\Bigr(\sum_{i:d(i,1)\leq cN^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}u_{i}^{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{H}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying (
4.3
),
\alpha>0
,
\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}
be as in (
4.2
) and
U_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. We denote

\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}}=\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}\mathrm{H}\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}^{-1}-\mathrm{H}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}(\mathbb{R}).

For each
1\leq i\leq N
, one may split
(\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}}U_{N})_{i}
into

(4.5)

(\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}}U_{N})_{i}=\underbrace{\sum_{l:d_{N}(i,l)\leq\frac{1}{2}d_{N}(i,1)}(\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}})_{i,l}u_{l}}_{({I})_{i}}+\underbrace{\sum_{l:d_{N}(i,l)>\frac{1}{2}d_{N}(i,1)}(\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}})_{i,l}u_{l}}_{({II})_{i}}.

If
d_{N}(i,l)\leq\frac{1}{2}d_{N}(i,1)
, then

\Bigr|\frac{\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{l}}{\gamma_{l}}\Bigr|\leq C\frac{d_{N}(i,l)}{1+d_{N}(i,1)}

and it follows from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

(4.6)

|({I})_{i}|\leq\frac{Cn^{\varepsilon}}{d_{N}(i,1)^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}|U_{N}|.

Let us choose
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{1}{2})
. If
d_{N}(i,l)\geq\frac{1}{2}d_{N}(i,1)
, then

\Bigr|\frac{\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{l}}{\gamma_{l}}\Bigr|\leq C\frac{\gamma_{i}}{\gamma_{l}},

which gives, since
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
,

(4.7)

|({II})_{i}|\leq Cn^{\varepsilon}d_{N}(i,1)^{\alpha-s}\sum_{l:d_{N}(i,l)>\frac{1}{2}d_{N}(i,1)}\frac{1}{d_{N}(l,1)^{\alpha}}|u_{l}|\leq\frac{Cn^{\varepsilon}}{d_{N}(i,1)^{s-\alpha}}|U_{N}|.

Let
K_{0}\geq 1
. Combining (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) one obtains

|U_{N}\cdot\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}}U_{N}|\leq CN^{\varepsilon}|U_{N}|^{2}\Bigr(\sum_{i:d_{N}(i,1)\geq K_{0}}\frac{1}{d_{N}(i,1)^{2\min(s-\frac{1}{2},s-\alpha)}}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}+CN^{\varepsilon}|U_{N}|\Bigr(\sum_{i:d_{N}(i,1)\leq K_{0}}u_{i}^{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
\leq CN^{\varepsilon}|U_{N}|^{2}\frac{1}{K_{0}^{\min(s-1,\mathrm{s}-\frac{1}{2}-\alpha)}}+CN^{\varepsilon}|U_{N}|\Bigr(\sum_{i:d_{N}(i,1)\leq K_{0}}u_{i}^{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Therefore by choosing
K_{0}=cN^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
with
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{0}}{\min(s-1,s-\frac{1}{2}-\alpha)}
, we find that

(4.8)

|U_{N}\cdot\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}}U_{N}|\leq\frac{1}{2}N^{-\varepsilon_{0}}|U_{N}|^{2}+CN^{\varepsilon}|U_{N}|\Bigr(\sum_{i:d_{N}(i,1)\leq K_{0}}u_{i}^{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

∎

4.2.
Localization in a smaller window

Due to the degeneracy of the interaction at infinity, the system lacks of uniform convexity and one shall sometimes restrict the system to a smaller window. Fix
n
to be the size of a subsystem, say
n=N
or
n\leq N/2
, and let
I=\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Using the Log-Sobolev inequality, one may add some convexity within the window
I
without changing much the measure. Denote
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{N}\to\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
the projection on the coordinates
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
. For
\varepsilon>0
and
\theta:[0,+\infty)\to(0,+\infty)
smooth such that
\theta=0
on
(1,+\infty)
,
\theta^{\prime\prime}\geq 1
on
[0,\frac{1}{2})
,
\theta^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
on
[0,+\infty]
, let us define

(4.9)

\mathrm{F}^{\mathrm{g}}:X_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta(n^{-\varepsilon}x_{i})

and the constrained measure

(4.10)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}_{N,\beta}^{\mathrm{g}}\propto e^{-\beta\mathrm{F}^{\mathrm{g}}\circ\pi}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}^{\mathrm{g}}.

Note that the forcing (
4.9
) is tuned so that the total variation distance between
\mathbb{P}_{N,\beta}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{N,\beta}
decays exponentially in
n
in view of the Log-Sobolev inequality. We now define

(4.11)

\nu:=\mathbb{Q}_{N,\beta}^{\mathrm{g}}\circ\pi^{-1}.

Let also

(4.12)

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}:x\in\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N})\mapsto-\frac{1}{\beta}\log\int e^{-\beta(\mathcal{H}_{N-n}^{\mathrm{g}}(y)+\mathcal{H}_{n,N}^{\mathrm{g}}(x,y))}\mathrm{d}y,

where

(4.13)

\mathcal{H}_{n,N}^{\mathrm{g}}:(x,y)\in(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-n})\cap\mathcal{M}_{N}\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{g}}(x,y)-\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\mathrm{g}}(x)-\mathcal{H}_{N-n}^{\mathrm{g}}(y).

By Lemma
3.5
,
\nu
may be written in the form

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu(x)

\displaystyle\propto e^{-\beta\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\mathrm{g}}(x)}\mathds{1}_{\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N})}(x)\mathrm{d}x

where

(4.15)

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\mathrm{g}}:=\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\mathrm{g}}+\mathrm{F}^{\mathrm{g}}+\widetilde{E}.

In the sequel one will be studying the decay of the covariance between
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
under
\nu
through the analysis of the associated Helffer-Sjöstrand equation. Define the good event

(4.16)

\mathcal{A}=\left\{X_{n}\in\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N}):\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},n^{-\varepsilon}\leq x_{i}\leq n^{\varepsilon}\right\}\\
\cap\{\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},k\in\{1,\ldots,n-i\},|x_{i}+\ldots+x_{i+k-1}-k|\leq n^{\varepsilon}k^{\frac{1}{2}}\}.

Let
A=\nabla^{2}F(U_{n})\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
for some
U_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
where
F
is the quadratic form

F:X_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\sum_{i,j\in I}g_{s}^{\prime\prime}(j-i)(x_{i}+\ldots+x_{j})^{2}.

Let us decompose
\nabla^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\mathrm{g}}
into
\nabla^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\mathrm{g}}=\mathsf{M}+M
with

(4.17)

\mathsf{M}=\nabla^{2}\mathrm{F}^{\mathrm{g}}+\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\mathrm{g}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}}+A\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}^{c}}\quad\text{and}\quad M=\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\mathrm{g}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}^{c}}-A\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}^{c}}+\nabla^{2}\widetilde{E}.

In the case
n\leq N/2
, we will replace
\nabla^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\mathrm{g}}
in (
4.1
) by
\mathsf{M}
and derive some decay estimates on the solution, which will be transferred to the solution of (
4.1
) by a convexity argument. One can check that uniformly on the event (
4.16
) and for each
1\leq i,j\leq n
, we have

(4.18)

|M_{i,j}|\leq\frac{Cn^{\kappa\varepsilon}}{1+d_{N}(i,\partial I)^{s-1/2}d_{N}(j,\partial I)^{s-1/2}}.

Until the end of the section we let
d
be the symmetric distance on
\{1,\ldots,N\}
if
n=N
or the usual distance on
\{1,\ldots,n\}
if
n\leq\frac{N}{2}
:

(4.19)

d:(i,j)\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^{2}\mapsto\begin{cases}\min(|j-i|,N-|j-i|)&\text{if $n=N$}\\
|j-i|&\text{if $n\leq\frac{N}{2}$}.\end{cases}

For the purpose of Section
6
it is convenient to work with a general measure
\nu
on
\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N})
satisfying the following:

Assumptions 4.1
.

Let
\nu
be a probability measure on
\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N})
in the form
\mathrm{d}\nu=e^{-\beta H^{\mathrm{g}}(x)}\mathrm{d}x
with
H^{\mathrm{g}}:\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N})\to\mathbb{R}
in
\mathcal{C}^{2}
and such that

\lim_{d(x,\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N}))\to 0}\nabla H^{\mathrm{g}}(x)\cdot\vec{n}=-\infty.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be the good event (
4.16
). Assume that there exist
C>0,\delta>0
(depending on
\varepsilon
) such that

\nu(\mathcal{A}^{c})\leq Ce^{-n^{\delta}}.

Note that the above condition ensures first that no boundary term appears in the computations and second that the Langevin dynamics is conservative, implying that the maximum principle of Proposition
3.6
holds true.

Instead of the specific interaction matrix defined in (
4.17
) we will be working with a more general measurable function
\mathsf{M}
from
\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N})
to
\mathcal{S}_{n_{0}}(\mathbb{R})
with
n_{0}\leq n
satisfying the following:

Assumptions 4.2
.

Let
n_{0}\leq n
. Let
\mathsf{M}
be a measurable map from
\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N})
to
\mathcal{S}_{n_{0}}(\mathbb{R})
.

(1)

There exists
\kappa>0
such that uniformly on
\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N})
,

\mathsf{M}\geq n^{-\kappa\varepsilon}I_{n_{0}}.

(2)

There exist
\kappa>0
and
C>0
such that uniformly on
\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N})
and for each
1\leq i,j\leq n_{0}
,

|\mathsf{M}_{i,j}|\leq\frac{Cn^{\kappa\varepsilon}}{1+d(i,j)^{s}},

where
d
is as in (
4.19
).

4.3.
The initial decay estimate

In this subsection we introduce a simple perturbation argument, which gives a first estimate on the decay of correlations for the constrained hypersingular Riesz gas. The method can be applied to other convex models for which the Hessian of the energy satisfies some decay assumption. This technique follows from an adaptation of a rather classical argument in statistical physics
[
HS94
,
CT73
]
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
s\in(1,+\infty).
Let
\nu
and
\mathsf{M}
satisfying Assumptions
4.1
and
4.2
. Let
\chi_{n}\in H^{1}(\nu),i_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
\psi\in L^{2}(I,H^{1}(\nu))
be the solution of

(4.20)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\beta\mathsf{M}\psi+\mathcal{L}^{\nu}\psi=\chi_{n}e_{i_{0}}&\text{on }\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N})\\
\psi\cdot\vec{n}=0&\text{on }\partial\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N}).\end{array}\right.

Let
d
be as in (
4.19
). Then, for all
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{1}{2})
, there exist a constant
C(\beta)
locally uniform in
\beta
and
\kappa>0
such that

(4.21)

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\Bigr[\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(i,i_{0})^{2\alpha}\psi_{i}^{2}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C(\beta)n^{\kappa\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\chi_{n}^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

Let
\psi\in L^{2}(I,H^{1}(\nu))
be in the solution of (
4.20
). Taking the scalar product of (
4.20
) with
\psi
and integrating by parts, one may show that there exist constants
\kappa>0
and
C>0
such that

(4.22)

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[|\nabla\psi|^{2}]+\beta\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[|\psi|^{2}]\leq C\beta^{-1}n^{\kappa\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\chi_{n}^{2}].

Fix
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{1}{2})
and consider as in (
4.2
) the distortion matrix

\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}=\mathrm{diag}(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}),\quad\text{where $\gamma_{i}=1+d(i,i_{0})^{\alpha}$ for each $1\leq i\leq n$}.

Let us define
u^{\mathrm{dis}}
the distorted vector-field

(4.23)

u^{\mathrm{dis}}:=\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}u\in L^{2}(I,H^{1}(\nu)).

Observing that
\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}e_{i_{0}}=e_{i_{0}}
, we can check that
u^{\mathrm{dis}}
solves

(4.24)

A_{1}^{\nu}u^{\mathrm{dis}}+\beta\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}}u^{\mathrm{dis}}=\chi_{n}e_{i_{0}},

where

\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}}:=\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}\mathsf{M}\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}^{-1}-\mathsf{M}.

Taking the scalar product of (
4.24
) with
u^{\mathrm{dis}}
and integrating by parts under
\nu
gives

(4.25)

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\beta u^{\mathrm{dis}}\cdot(\mathsf{M}+\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}})u^{\mathrm{dis}}]+\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[|\nabla u^{\mathrm{dis}}|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[u_{i_{0}}\chi_{n}],

where we have used the fact that
u^{\mathrm{dis}}_{i_{0}}=u_{i_{0}}
. This gives

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\beta u^{\mathrm{dis}}\cdot(\mathsf{M}+\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}})u^{\mathrm{dis}}]+\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[|\nabla u^{\mathrm{dis}}|^{2}]\leq C(\beta)n^{\kappa_{0}\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\chi_{n}^{2}].

By assumption, there exist constants
C>0,\kappa>0
such that uniformly on
D_{N}
and for each
i\neq j
,

|\mathsf{M}_{i,j}|\leq\frac{Cn^{\kappa\varepsilon}}{1+d(i,j)^{s}}.

One may therefore apply Lemma
4.1
to the matrix
\mathrm{H}=\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\mathrm{g}}(X_{n})
, which gives the existence of
\kappa>0
and
\kappa^{\prime}>0
independent of
X_{n}
such that, letting

K_{0}=\lfloor n^{\kappa\varepsilon}\rfloor,

there holds

(4.26)

|\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[u^{\mathrm{dis}}\cdot\delta_{\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}}u^{\mathrm{dis}}]|\leq\frac{n^{-\varepsilon(s+2)}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[|u^{\mathrm{dis}}|^{2}]-C(\beta)n^{\kappa^{\prime}\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[|u^{\mathrm{dis}}|^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\Bigr[\sum_{i:d(i,i_{0})\leq K_{0}}(u^{\mathrm{dis}}_{i})^{2}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Furthermore, using the definition of
u^{\mathrm{dis}}
(
4.23
) and the a priori bound (
4.22
), we find that

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\Bigr[\sum_{i:d(i,i_{0})\leq K_{0}}(u^{\mathrm{dis}}_{i})^{2}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq K_{0}^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[|u|^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C(\beta)n^{\kappa^{\prime\prime}\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\chi_{n}^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Combining these we deduce that there exists
\kappa>0
such that

(4.27)

\frac{\beta}{2}n^{-\varepsilon(s+2)}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\Bigr[\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(i,i_{0})^{2\alpha}(\psi_{i}^{(1)})^{2}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\Bigr[\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(i,i_{0})^{2\alpha}|\nabla\psi_{i}^{(l)}|^{2}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C(\beta)n^{\kappa\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\chi_{n}^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

∎

4.4.
Bootstrap on the decay exponent

This subsection introduces an iterative argument to improve the decay estimate of Lemma
4.2
. The method consists in studying the projection of Equation (
4.20
) in a small window. By controlling the field outside the window with the a priori decay estimate, one obtains through the distortion argument of Lemma
4.2
a better decay estimate on the solution. After a finite number of iterations one gets the following optimal result:

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
s\in(1,+\infty).
Let
\nu
and
\mathsf{M}
satisfying Assumptions
4.1
and
4.2
. Let
\chi_{n}\in H^{1}(\nu),i_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
\psi\in L^{2}(I,H^{1}(\nu))
be the solution of

(4.28)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\beta\mathsf{M}\psi+\mathcal{L}^{\nu}\psi=\chi_{n}e_{i_{0}}&\text{on }\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N})\\
\psi\cdot\vec{n}=0&\text{on }\partial\pi(\mathcal{M}_{N}).\end{array}\right.

Let
d
be as in (
4.19
). There exist
\kappa>0
and
C(\beta)>0
locally uniform in
\beta
such that for each
1\leq j\leq n
,

(4.29)

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\psi_{j}^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C(\beta)n^{\kappa\varepsilon}\frac{1}{1+d(j,i_{0})^{s}}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\chi_{n}^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

Step 1: setting the bootstrap

Assume that for any
n_{0}\leq n
and all
\mathsf{M}
